## Page 1


GLOBAL DYNAMICS OF VISCOUS GASEOUS STARS IN A PHYSICAL
VACUUM
DEMIN WANG, JIAWEN ZHANG, AND SHENGGUO ZHU
Abstract. The study of vacuum is important in understanding compressible flows.
In
particular, physical vacuum, in which the boundary moves with a nontrivial finite normal
acceleration, naturally arises in the study of the motion of gaseous stars. In this paper, we
analyze the free boundary problem for the three-dimensional compressible Navier–Stokes–
Poisson equations with degenerate viscosities for self-gravitating viscous gaseous stars. For
the spherically symmetric and barotropic motion, we establish the global well-posedness
of classical solutions without any restriction on the size of the initial data. Our solutions
obtained here are smooth all the way up to the moving boundary and capture the physical
vacuum boundary behavior of the Lane–Emden star configuration.
1. Introduction
The motion of a self-gravitating viscous gaseous star in the universe can be described
by the following vacuum free boundary problem (VFBP) of the three-dimensional (3-D)
compressible Navier–Stokes–Poisson equations (CNSP):

Moreover, the solutions obtained here are smooth all the way up to the moving boundary.
1.1. Background of the problem. The study on the dynamics of vacuum is crucial for
understanding compressible flows. Actually, the presence of vacuum leads to a degeneracy
in the time evolution of hydrodynamic equations, which poses substantial challenges to the
analysis of well-posedness theory; see [9,22,24,28] and the references therein. It is noteworthy
that, from a physics perspective, the concept of fluid velocity loses meaning in the absence
of fluid. Moreover, the underlying assumption in the derivation of hydrodynamic equations
from physical principles is that the fluid is nondilute and can be described as a continuum,
which breaks down in vacuum regions, rendering hydrodynamic equations inapplicable for
describing the time evolution of thermodynamic states there. Based on this consideration,
the free boundary problem has garnered considerable attention for tracking vacuum problems
of compressible flows. As in (1.1), such formulation of vacuum problems requires that the
hydrodynamic equations hold only on the domain
{(t, x) : ρ(t, x) > 0},
together with an evolution equation for the boundary ∂Ω(t) which separates the fluids and
vacuum. A key feature of VFBP is that ∂Ω(t) propagates with finite speed when ρ0 is of
compact support. For the physical relevance of this phenomenon, we refer the reader to the
survey by Nishida [29] and the references therein.


## Page 3


VISCOUS GASEOUS STARS
3
Due to the high degeneracy in the time evolution of hydrodynamic equations caused by
the behavior (1.8) near the moving boundary, the well-posedness of classical solutions to the
corresponding free boundary problem becomes extremely challenging for both the inviscid and
viscous compressible flow. For VFBP of the barotropic compressible Euler equations, a series
of important progress has been made on the well-posedness of smooth solutions satisfying
(1.8).
The local well-posedness theory was developed by Coutand–Shkoller [7] and Jang–
Masmoudi [16], respectively. Hadˇzi´c–Jang [12] constructed global unique 3-D solutions for
adiabatic exponents γ ∈(1, 5
3], provided that the initial data are sufficiently close to the
expanding compactly supported affine motions constructed by Sideris [36] and satisfy (1.8).
Later, by a different approach, Shkoller-Sideris [35] proved the stability of affine motions and
global existence of classical solutions for all γ > 1. On the other hand, for the VFBP of 3-D
barotropic compressible Euler–Poisson equations, the local well-posedness theory of classical
solutions satisfying (1.8) was established by Gu–Lei [10], and the global well-posedness of
classical solutions was obtained by Hadˇzi´c–Jang [14], which stay close to Sideris affine solutions
of the Euler equations. Some other important developments can be found in [6,11,13,21,23,
25,31,34,38] and the references therein.
For the corresponding VFBP of viscous compressible flows in a physical vacuum (as de-
fined in (1.8)), under the assumption that the viscosity coefficients are all constants, i.e., δ = 0
in (1.6), Jang [15] established the local well-posedness of strong solutions to CNSP in the
spherically symmetric and barotropic motion. When the viscosity coefficient obeys a power
law of ρ (i.e., ρδ with some exponent δ ∈(0, 1) in (1.6)), for CNSP in a physical vacuum,
Wang–Zhang–Zhu [39] proved the local well-posedness of spherically symmetric classical solu-
tions, and Luo–Xin–Zeng [26] established the global existence of spherically symmetric strong
solutions under some smallness assumptions. On the other hand, recently, in Chen–Zhang–
Zhu [5], the global well-posedness of classical solutions in a physical vacuum to VFBP of the
viscous Saint–Venant system has been established in the spherically symmetric motion. Some
other important developments can be found in [19,30,33] and the references therein.
Despite these key progresses on the well-posedness theory of VFBP for compressible fluids,
for the physically important case that δ ∈[0, 1) in (1.6), no matter for the compressible Navier–
Stokes equations or CNSP, the corresponding global well-posedness of classical solutions with
large data and physical vacuum in multi-dimensional space remains open. Nevertheless, to
gain deeper insight into the dynamics of viscous gaseous stars near their moving boundary or
the center, e.g., whether the moving boundary of the star always expands with finite speed
in positive time, or whether a collapse will occur at the center of the star in finite time, it is
highly desirable to establish the global regularity of solutions with general smooth data for
the corresponding VFBP of CNSP.

where ▲denotes the coordinate singularity, ⊛the degenerate time evolution, and 3 the
degenerate spatial dissipation. For establishing the global well-posedness of classical solutions
with general data, our analysis encounters the following major obstacles:
• Possible cavitation or implosion within the fluids;
• Possible degeneracy of the coordinate transformation between the Eulerian and La-
grangian coordinates.
Dealing with these two obstacles is challenging due to the combined difficulties of
(i) Coordinate singularity ▲at the origin, manifested by the singular factor 1
η in (1.22);
(ii) Strong degeneracy in both time evolution ⊛and spatial dissipation 3 near the vacuum;
(iii) Lack of uniform positive lower and upper bounds of (ηr, η
r );
(iv) Strong coupling between the Navier–Stokes equations and Poisson equation;
(v) Strong nonlinear dependence of the viscosity coefficients on ρ (i.e., δ ∈(13
18, 1) in (1.6)).
Thus, new ideas are required on the large data problems of VFBP (1.1) with physical
vacuum. Fortunately, by exploiting the intrinsic degenerate-singular structure of CNSP in
Lagrangian coordinates (t, r), and assuming that (1.21) holds, we can prove the global well-
posedness of classical solutions of VFBP (1.1) with physical vacuum and general data in the
spherically symmetric motion.

physical vacuum boundary behavior of the Lane–Emden star configurations for all γ ∈(4
3, 3).

The rest of this paper is organized as follows: In §2, we outline the main strategy for
proving Theorem 1.1. §3–§5 are devoted to deriving the desired global uniform estimates for
solutions in the purpose-built function spaces. With these estimates in hand, we obtain the
desired global well-posedness of classical solutions in §6 by continuation arguments. At last,
some basic lemmas, useful coordinate transformations for spherically symmetric functions,
and remarks on the initial conditions are collected in Appendices A–B.
2. Notations and Main Strategies
In this section, we first present some notations in §2.1, which will be used throughout this
paper. In §2.2, we show the main strategies of our analysis.

2.2. Main strategies. Now we sketch the main strategy of our analysis.
2.2.1. Selection of the suitable energy functionals. In the study on the spherically symmetric
motion in a physical vacuum of viscous gaseous stars, we need to deal with the combined
difficulties of the physical vacuum singularity on the moving boundary, the coordinate singu-
larity at the center, and the nonlinearity of the physical viscosity coefficients, which makes it
intricate to provide an effective propagation mechanism for the regularity of the fluid velocity.
Then the first key point in our analysis is to introduce a proper weighted energy functional.
In other words, what kind of function space should we construct the solution in? Here, we
select the energy functionals in the neighborhoods of the origin and the moving boundary
separately, and then combine them appropriately to obtain uniform estimates for the velocity.
First, the interior energy functionals (Ein, Din) are chosen based on the 3-D Lagrangian
spatial coordinates (t, y). If we let U(t, y) := U(t, r)y
r , then Ein(t, U) + tDin(t, U) ∈L∞(0, T)
can be read as the following Hk-norms of U due to Lemma B.1 in Appendix B:

2.2.2. Global uniform upper and lower bounds of (ηr, η
r ). To deal with the moving boundary,
we transformed the VFBP (1.1) into the IBVP (1.19) in a fixed domain by introducing
the Lagrangian coordinates.
To determine the mathematical structure of the momentum
equations in Lagrangian coordinates, we must ensure that the coordinate transformation
between the Eulerian and Lagrangian coordinates is nondegenerate. Thus, the major task
before establishing the uniform estimates is to obtain the uniform upper and lower bounds of
(ηr, η
r ). The procedure of the proof can be roughly summarized in Figure 1.
The main ingredient of our analysis consists of different types of weighted estimates adapted
to the exterior region (near the moving vacuum boundary) and the interior region (near the
origin). On one hand, in the exterior, we establish the well-designed (ρ0, ηr)-estimates for
velocity U to overcome the physical vacuum singularity on the moving boundary; these play
an important role in obtaining the exterior upper bound for (ηr, η
r ). On the other hand, in
the interior, we handle the coordinate singularity at the symmetry center by employing the
(η, ηr)-weighted estimates for ϱ, which yield the interior lower bound of (ηr, η
r ), as well as
ηr-weighted estimates for both (ϱ, U), which yield the interior upper bound of (ηr, η
r ). The
two types of estimates are treated separately using different cut-off functions and are then
carefully combined to obtain the desired global estimates.

Such a local well-posedness has been proven in [39]. Since in the current paper, as defined

task is to establish the global uniform estimates of (U, η) in function spaces (E, D)(t, U), and
then we can extend the local solutions to be global ones by standard continuation arguments.
Following the same proof as that of Theorem 3.1, presented in [39, Section 3], we can show
that (U, η) is a classical solution of (1.25) in (0, T] × ¯I for any time T > 0.
Throughout §3–§5, we always assume the following:
• (β, δ, γ) satisfy β = γ −1 and (1.34);
• (U, η)(t, r) is a classical solution of IBVP (1.25) in [0, T] × ¯I for some T > 0, which
is constructed in Theorem 3.1;
• C0 ∈(1, ∞) is a generic constant depending only on (a1, A, γ, δ, ε0, ρ0, u0, K1, K2)
and C(l1, · · ·, lk) ∈(1, ∞) is a generic constant depending on C0, G, and parameters
(l1, · · ·, lk), which may be different at each occurrence, i.e.,
C0 = C0(a1, A, γ, δ, ε0, ρ0, u0, K1, K2),
C(l1, · · ·, lk) = C(l1, · · ·, lk, C0, G).
In particular, C(l1, · · ·, lk) is continuous and strictly increasingly with respect to G.

6. Global Well-Posedness of the Classical Solutions
Now we are ready to give the proof for Theorem 1.1.
According to Theorem 3.1, there exists a unique classical solution (U, η)(t, r) of IBVP
(1.25) in [0, T∗] × ¯I for some T∗> 0, satisfying (3.2) and (1.37). Now, suppose that T ∗> 0
is the life span of (U, η)(t, r), and T is any fixed time satisfying T ∈(0, T ∗). We claim that
T ∗= ∞.
Otherwise, if T ∗< ∞, collecting the uniform a priori bounds obtained in Lemmas 3.12,

solution (U, η)(t, r) in [0, T] × ¯I such that (1.36) holds.
Appendix A. Some Basic Lemmas
For the convenience of readers, we list some basic results that have been used in this paper.
The first lemma concerns the separability and density of the weighted Sobolev spaces.
Lemma A.1 ([17]). Let ϑ1, ϑ2 ∈(−1, ∞), and let w(r) ∼rϑ1(1 −r)ϑ2 be a function defined
on I. Then, for k ∈Z, Hk

is dense in Hk
w with respect to its norm.
The second lemma is on the classical Sobolev embedding theorems.
Lemma A.2 ([18]). We state the Sobolev embedding theorem in R and R3 separately.

Appendix B. Coordinate Transformations
Generally, it is desirable to consider the Lagrangian formulation, so that we can pullback
(1.1) on the moving domain Ω(t) to a problem on a fixed domain Ω. To this end, denote by
x = η(t, y) the position of the fluid particle x ∈Ω(t) at time t ≥0 so that
ηt(t, y) = u(t, η(t, y)) for t > 0,
with η(0, y) = y,
(B.1)
and (t, y) are the 3-D Lagrangian coordinates.
This appendix is devoted to showing the
conversion of some Sobolev spaces between the 3-D Lagrangian coordinates y and the cor-
responding spherical coordinate r = |y| for spherically symmetric functions. This appendix
is devoted to showing the conversion of some Sobolev spaces between the M-D coordinates y
and the spherical coordinate r = |y| for spherically symmetric functions.
